Fixed with a new, narrower function, `touchSourceListingSeen` (`src/db/write-source-listing-revision.ts`): records that a listing was seen in the index without a successful content fetch behind it — bumps `lastSeenAt`/resets `missingStreak` and reactivates a `missing_suspected` listing (which already has prior real content) but leaves a bare `discovered` listing `discovered` (no content yet, so no "active" claim) and never reopens `closed`/`expired` (that needs the stronger confirmed-reappearance evidence below). The orchestrator calls it on both failure branches.
  - **Reappearing listings never reopened.** Concept §13's lifecycle explicitly allows `closed`/`expired` → `active` on reappearance, and `crawl_runs.reopenedCount` existed from Phase 0 but nothing ever set it. `writeSourceListingRevision` deliberately never reactivated `closed`/`expired` on its own (a stray fetch from anywhere isn't strong enough evidence) — rather than changing that default (and its existing, already-reviewed tests), added an explicit opt-in: `options.allowReopen`, plus a `reopened` result field. The orchestrator passes `allowReopen: true`, since a full discovery walk that re-found and successfully re-parsed a listing's content *is* the confirmed-reappearance signal the original design withheld. `reopenedCount` is now tracked for real.
  - **Tests could have deleted real jobs.ge data.** `crawl.test.ts` originally ran every scenario against the real, fixed `jobsGeSource.id`, so `afterEach`'s `cleanupTestSource(jobsGeSource.id)` would delete every real jobs.ge row a shared dev database might already hold — the exact opposite of every other test file's random-UUID isolation. Fixed via `vi.mock('../../policies/jobs-ge.js', ...)` overriding only the `id`/`sourceId` fields to a random per-test-file UUID (baseUrl and the rest of the policy stay real, so URL construction and `isJobsGeUrlAllowed` behave exactly as in production) — plus a permanent regression assertion that `jobsGeSource.id` is never the real constant during tests.

  A second adversarial review pass returned 2 more P1s (P2s skipped per the project's severity-gate policy), both fixed:
  - **Overlapping crawls could double-count missing streaks.** Two runs for the same source in flight at once (a scheduler firing while a slow previous run is still going) could each independently reconcile the same absent listing against their own later `startedAt`, advancing `missingStreak` twice off overlapping observations rather than genuinely consecutive ones. Fixed at the database level, not with an application-side lock a connection pool would make unreliable to hold for a run's full (potentially hours-long) duration: a partial unique index, `crawl_runs_one_running_per_source_idx` (migration 0009), allows at most one `status = 'running'` row per source. `startCrawlRun` (`src/db/ingest.ts`) catches that specific constraint violation and raises a typed `CrawlAlreadyRunningError` — callers treat it as "skip this invocation," not a failure, since no run was actually created. Known, accepted limitation: a process that crashes hard enough to skip its own `finally`-style `finishCrawlRun('failed')` cleanup leaves a stale `running` row that blocks all future runs for that source until manually cleared — real stale-lock recovery is Phase 7 (operational telemetry/incident workflows) territory, not Phase 1A's.
  - **Detail-page parse failures weren't typed or quarantined.** concept §26's acceptance criteria explicitly require this, and folding a parse failure (the fetch succeeded; our parser couldn't extract the content — a markup/template mismatch) into the same `failedCount` as an ordinary network failure meant even a site-wide parser break would just look like a routine, mildly elevated failure rate. Fixed with `quarantineSourceListing` (`src/db/write-source-listing-revision.ts`, unconditionally overrides status to `quarantined` regardless of prior state — unreliable evidence takes precedence over any lifecycle nuance — and is itself excluded from every other function's reactivation allowlist here until a human resolves it) and `recordParserIncident` (`src/db/ingest.ts`, writes a `parser_incidents` row — the durable record concept §22's supervised-repair flow expects). The orchestrator's parse-failure branch now quarantines the listing and its resource, records the incident (kind `field_missing`, severity `warning`, evidence including the parser version and raw error), and counts it via `quarantinedCount` — distinct from `failedCount`, which is now fetch-failures only.

  A third adversarial review pass returned 2 more P1s (again, P2s skipped per policy), both fixed:
  - **A single repeated page wasn't strong enough evidence of the real clamp.** Round 1's fix (compare consecutive pages' own ID sets) still trusted a lone repeat — a transient cache/proxy glitch serving the previous page's content again would satisfy it just as well as genuine terminal pagination, and with the 100-listing floor, a coincidence as early as page 2 could pass it while covering a tiny fraction of the real ~5,647-listing corpus. Fixed: a candidate stop is no longer trusted on its own — `discoverAllListings` now confirms it with an independent probe at `page + CLAMP_CONFIRMATION_PROBE_OFFSET` (20; RECON_NOTES.md confirmed jobs.ge's clamp behavior holds not just at the next page but at several far-apart out-of-range page numbers — 19's content repeated at 20, 21, *and* 50 — so this jump is evidenced, not arbitrary). Only if the probe's content also matches is the walk marked `complete`; an unconfirmed or failed probe is treated as a fluke and the walk resumes normally.
  - **A source-wide detail-parsing break wouldn't stop reconciliation.** `discoveryOk` only ever looked at discovery-page health (`complete`, the listing-count floor) — a run where discovery itself succeeded but the DETAIL template broke, quarantining most or all discovered listings, would still be marked `completed` and let `closeMissingListings` advance closure for listings genuinely absent from that same unreliable run. Fixed: `discoveryOk` now also requires the quarantine rate (`quarantinedCount / discoveredCount`) to stay under `DEFAULT_MAX_QUARANTINE_RATE` (10%, tolerating ordinary per-listing noise while catching a genuine site-wide break) — computed after the per-listing loop finishes, since it needs the final `quarantinedCount`, unlike the other two conditions which are known right after discovery.

  A fourth adversarial review pass returned 1 more P1 (3 P2s skipped per policy — deadline-day expiry rounding, a fetch-failure-rate anomaly gate, and using `quarantined` rather than `partial` for the run status when the quarantine-rate gate trips), fixed:
  - **Even a confirmed clamp couldn't rule out a systemic pagination/caching regression.** Round 3's confirmation probe closes the "one repeated page" gap, but not a *consistent* one: if a bug or intermediary served the same 100-300 listings at every page number queried — including the distant probe — `complete`, the fixed floor, and the quarantine rate could all pass while the real ~5,647-listing corpus had effectively collapsed, none of them knowing what this source's corpus is actually supposed to look like. Fixed with `getLastCompletedCrawlRun` (`src/db/ingest.ts`): `discoveryOk` now also requires this run's `discoveredCount` to be at least `DEFAULT_MIN_RELATIVE_COVERAGE_RATIO` (50%) of the source's own most recent completed run — a check against real history a fixed floor can't express. Never blocks a source's first-ever run, which has no prior baseline to compare against.

  A fifth adversarial review pass returned 1 more P1, fixed:
  - **A single-partition collapse was invisible to the combined-total checks.** VIP is only ~10 of jobs.ge's ~5,647-listing corpus (RECON_NOTES.md), so `.vipEntries` silently breaking (e.g. the site changes that container's markup) would barely move `discoveredCount` — passing the fixed floor and the relative-collapse ratio easily, then letting every prior VIP listing quietly accumulate `missingStreak` toward closure over the following runs, exactly the failure concept §26's "VIP and standard sections are measured separately" acceptance criterion exists to catch. Fixed by persisting the breakdown for real: new `crawl_runs.vip_count`/`standard_count` columns (migration 0010, `CrawlRunSchema`/`CrawlRunCounts` updated to match) computed from each listing's already-known `.partition` tag. `discoveryOk` now also requires that if the last completed run had a non-zero count for a partition, this run's count for that same partition is non-zero too — deliberately a "went from something to nothing" check, not a fixed floor or ratio: VIP is small and genuinely variable (a source can legitimately have zero active VIP slots on a given day), so only a transition from a real prior baseline to zero is treated as suspicious, never "is currently small."

  A sixth adversarial review pass returned 1 more P1 (a P2 about avoiding repeated false reopen/expire churn for a still-indexed-past-deadline listing was skipped per policy), fixed:
  - **The exclusivity lock released before reconciliation actually committed.** Round 2's fix keyed the partial unique index on `status = 'running'` — but the first `finishCrawlRun` call flips `status` to its terminal value (so `closeMissingListings` can read it) *before* `expireOverdueListings`/`closeMissingListings` run, which silently released the lock for the entire window between "status set" and "reconciliation committed." A second invocation starting in that exact window could race the first run's still-in-flight reconciliation, reintroducing round 2's original double-counting bug in a narrower form — and a crash in that same window left a row that looked `completed` (a successful-looking run!) whose reconciliation had never actually happened. Fixed by decoupling the two concerns that `status` was conflating: a new `crawl_runs.reconciled_at` column (migration 0011) now backs the exclusivity index (`crawl_runs_one_unsettled_per_source_idx`, replacing the old status-keyed one) and is set only once a run is truly settled — at the *second* `finishCrawlRun` call (after reconciliation commits) for a normal run, or immediately in the `catch` block for a `'failed'` run, which never reaches reconciliation and never will. `status` still flips to its terminal value at the first call, unchanged — `closeMissingListings`'s own contract (round 2) didn't need to change at all.

  A seventh adversarial review pass returned 1 more P1 (a P2 about `crawl.ts` collapsing the discovered `href` and its resolved absolute form into the same `originalUrl`/`canonicalUrl` value, losing raw-provenance fidelity, was skipped per policy), fixed:
  - **Migration 0011 would fail or permanently wedge a source with real history.** Adding `reconciled_at` as a plain nullable column with no backfill left every pre-existing row `NULL` — for a source with more than one historical run, creating the new partial unique index over those duplicate `NULL` keys would fail migration outright; with exactly one, it would apply silently but leave that source locked out of every future crawl (its one historical row permanently "occupying" the unsettled slot). Fixed by backfilling `reconciled_at = finished_at` for every already-terminal (non-`'running'`) row before creating the index — safe because any such row settled before this column existed, well before the race this migration protects against was even possible. Verified directly, not just reasoned about: applied migrations 0000-0010 by hand against a scratch database, inserted two historical `completed`/`failed` rows for one source (simulating exactly the scenario above), applied the corrected 0011, confirmed both rows backfilled correctly, and confirmed a new crawl for that source could start immediately after.

  An eighth review pass — `/codex:adversarial-review --base main`, run whole-branch after opening the PR against `main` (2026-09-05), rather than the per-commit gate the first seven passes above went through — returned 3 highs and 1 medium, independently judged by a separate Opus-model review before acting on them (verifying each against the actual current code rather than trusting the report at face value) and all four confirmed real. Fixed:
  - **Quarantine was a one-way trapdoor.** `writeSourceListingRevision`'s `reopening` allowlist covered `closed`/`expired` but not `quarantined`, and no other function ever reactivated a quarantined listing either — so a single transient parse failure (any HTTP 200 that isn't the expected `.dtable` template: an interstitial, a challenge page, a truncated body — jobs.ge is already documented, `RECON_NOTES.md`, to return 200 for nonsense page numbers) permanently removed an otherwise-healthy listing from every future crawl, even though the very next run might parse it perfectly fine. Fixed by adding `quarantined` to the same `allowReopen`-gated allowlist `closed`/`expired` already use — a successful, non-stale re-parse within a full discovery walk is exactly the same "confirmed reappearance" evidence for a quarantined listing as it is for a closed one, and the orchestrator already passes `allowReopen: true`. Deliberately narrow: only the *listing's* status recovers; the underlying `parser_incidents` row's own resolution workflow (concept §22, supervised repair) stays out of scope, unchanged, for Phase 7. Proven both via the `changed`-path case (already covered by the existing `it.each(['closed','expired'])` reactivation test, now including `'quarantined'`) and, separately, the realistic `unchanged`-path case (a transient parse failure doesn't change the source's actual content, so recovery usually hashes identically to the pre-quarantine revision) plus an end-to-end `crawl.test.ts` scenario: quarantine on run 1, ordinary template on run 2, `reopenedCount === 1`.
  - **The unchanged-content write path never refreshed `sourcePublishedAt`/`sourceDeadlineAt` — attempted fix reverted after the per-commit gate caught its own regression.** `meaningfulContentHash` covers the RAW yearless date strings (`detail.ts`), not the parsed instant — `parseYearlessGeorgianDate` (`dates.ts`) resolves the SAME raw string to a different year as the reference (fetch) instant moves, since it picks whichever of `{refYear-1, refYear, refYear+1}` lands closest to that instant's own calendar date. Pinned directly: raw `02 აპრილი` resolves to 2026 as of a 2026-09-05 fetch but 2027 as of a 2026-11-01 fetch (`dates.test.ts`) — same raw string, same hash, different parsed instant, kept as a standalone regression pin on the date parser itself regardless of how the write path uses it. The first attempt at a fix (this same review pass) refreshed both denormalized columns on the unchanged path too, from the newly re-parsed `content` rather than the stale `currentRevision` — but the per-commit Codex gate caught a real regression this introduced before it could be committed: combined with `allowReopen` now covering `expired` (an existing behavior, not new to this pass — an expired-but-still-indexed listing reopens to `active` on any successful unchanged re-fetch, regardless of its deadline), refreshing the deadline from a fresh re-parse could silently roll an already-established, genuinely PAST deadline into next year's occurrence purely because real-world time had passed — no source change involved — leaving a truly expired listing wrongly `active` for months, contradicting concept §6.2's "prefer an explicit unknown state over an unsupported conclusion." That is a materially worse failure mode than the churn it was trying to fix (the round-6 P2 above: a still-past-deadline listing reopens then immediately re-expires again within the same run, which is cosmetically noisy — inflated `reopenedCount`/`expiredCount` — but never actually leaves the listing in a *wrong* end state). Reverted; the denormalized columns are left pinned to whatever the current, immutable revision established when it was created (`write-source-listing-revision.test.ts` now pins this explicitly), and the round-6 churn remains an accepted, deferred P2. A real fix needs to distinguish "time passed" from "the content or its interpretation actually changed" — e.g. gating a refresh on an actual `parserVersion` bump rather than a mere re-fetch — and is deliberately not attempted under this same review pass; left as future work.
  - **A skipped run (another crawl already in progress, or a stale unsettled lock from an earlier crash) exited 0.** `src/cli/run-jobs-ge-crawl.ts`'s `CrawlAlreadyRunningError` branch logged a warning and returned without setting `process.exitCode`, so Task Scheduler would record an indefinite series of silent "successes" — directly contradicting concept §19.1's "must not silently skip a run" and this same file's own header comment. The two triggering scenarios (a genuinely-overlapping run vs. an earlier run that crashed before ever reaching `reconciledAt`, permanently occupying the exclusivity slot — see round 2 and round 6 above) can't be told apart by this process alone, so both now log as an error and exit 1, with the message stating both readings and the exact recovery query (`update crawl_runs set status = 'failed', reconciled_at = now() where source_id = '<id>' and reconciled_at is null`) for the stale-lock case. Deliberately NOT adding lease/heartbeat/TTL semantics on top of the existing DB-row lock — stale-lock *auto*-recovery stays an accepted Phase 7 gap (round 2 above); inventing expiry logic now risks reintroducing the double-counted-missing-streak bug that lock exists to prevent. Verified by hand (no practical way to unit-test this file — it calls `main()` at import time and imports `db/client.ts`, which itself throws at import without `DATABASE_URL`): inserted an unsettled `crawl_runs` row directly, ran `npm run crawl:jobs-ge`, confirmed a non-zero exit code and the error log.
  - **The registered schedule's cadence didn't match what the crawl actually does.** `register-jobs-ge-schedule.ps1` defaulted to 60 minutes, citing concept §19.2's "jobs.ge lightweight discovery — 30-60 minutes" row — but `runJobsGeCrawl` doesn't do lightweight discovery-only polling; it's a full-corpus walk that refetches every listing's detail page every run (`fullCoverage: true`), which is §19.2's *"Complete source reconciliation"* row instead ("Nightly or weekly"). Measured basis: ~5,647 listings + ~21 discovery/probe fetches, at `jobsGePolicy`'s 5s crawl delay and `maxConcurrency: 1` (`src/policies/jobs-ge.ts`), is >= 7h50m of fetches alone per run — a 60-minute schedule would just run back-to-back continuously (`-MultipleInstances IgnoreNew` silently drops the overlapping triggers rather than queuing them) while claiming to be hourly. Confirming this does NOT require implementing §10.1's incremental discovery: that deferral stands as-is (see below) — true hourly-ish *discovery* is exactly what incremental discovery would unlock later; today's fix is just an honest cadence for the full-reconciliation crawl that exists now. Fixed by changing the default to 1440 minutes (nightly, the conservative end of that row) with the corrected citation in the script's own comments, plus a runtime warning if a caller overrides it below ~8 hours.

  A ninth review pass — a second `/codex:adversarial-review --base main` run after the eighth pass's fixes landed, using a different Codex model (`gpt-5.6-sol` vs. the eighth pass's model) — returned 3 more highs, again independently judged (Opus) before acting:
  - **Parser drift that preserved the title row but dropped everything else silently promoted a degraded revision.** `parseJobsGeDetailPage` (`src/adapters/jobs-ge/detail.ts`) only ever threw when the title (`დასახელება`) row was missing — organization/dates silently resolved to `null` and a missing description cell became `''` on ANY other structural drift, so a site template change that kept the title row but moved or renamed everything else produced a "successful" parse with a genuinely new hash (the raw HTML really did change) and got promoted to `currentRevisionId` with degraded data, invisible to the round-3 quarantine-rate guard since nothing threw — the one finding of this pass with NO existing safety net at all. Fixed with two narrow structural throws, both about a structure being ABSENT (never about a field's value being empty, which concept §6.2 explicitly permits as an honest unknown): the description cell (`table.dtable td:not(.dtitle)`) must exist, and at least one of the organization or published-date rows must be found. Deliberately "at least one of," not "both": RECON_NOTES.md's 18 sampled pages were sampled by ID recency, not stratified across the five announcement types this crawl deliberately aggregates (scholarships/trainings/tenders/other, not just vacancies), so there's no evidence every type carries an organization row — and this project's own synthetic orchestrator fixtures (`crawl.test.ts`'s `buildDetailHtml`) already have a published row with no organization row, which is itself evidence that requiring both would be over-tight and would risk quarantining an entire legitimate announcement type forever (worse than the drift being fixed). Proven with 3 new `detail.test.ts` cases (missing description cell; neither org nor published row; a negative control pinning that org-row absence alone must NOT throw) plus one `crawl.test.ts` end-to-end scenario: a healthy run establishes a revision, a second run serves title-only drift for the same listing, and the run is asserted `partial` with the listing quarantined and `currentRevisionId` UNCHANGED from the first run — last-known-good content preserved per §6.2, exactly as designed since round 8's quarantine-reopen fix.
  - Deliberately NOT attempted here, and recorded as a tracked gap instead: drift where the scaffold survives but a single label is silently renamed (e.g. organization alone → `null` while dates still parse fine) has no detection at all — catching it needs a persisted per-run field-presence baseline (the same shape as round 5's `vipCount`/`standardCount` collapse guard) to tell "this type never has an organization field" apart from "the organization row just broke," which needs a migration and evidence this pass didn't gather. A future pass should sketch that from real field-presence data across announcement types before implementing it, not from speculation.
  - **Total detail-fetch failure was reported as a completed run.** `discoveryOk`'s existing guards (completeness, the fixed floor, quarantine rate, relative-coverage baseline, per-partition VIP/standard collapse) never looked at `failedCount` — only at parse failures on pages that were actually fetched. So if discovery itself succeeded fully but every detail request failed (a ban/WAF challenge, systemic timeouts, an SSRF/policy block), `quarantineRate` stayed 0 (nothing got fetched to parse, so nothing threw), every other guard passed, and the run was marked `completed` despite acquiring zero real content that run — the CLI would exit 0 and Task Scheduler would report success. Independently judged: confirmed as a real gap, but lower severity than an initial read suggests, since `touchSourceListingSeen`'s existing `lastSeenAt` bump (round 8 above) already protects every discovered-but-unfetchable listing from looking missing regardless of run status — no mass closure was ever possible from this. Also a re-raise of an idea this project's own round 4 already logged (`docs/STATUS.md`, "a fetch-failure-rate anomaly gate") and deliberately skipped as a P2 at the time; taken now because a different review pass re-flagged it and the fix mirrors an already-reviewed pattern exactly. Fixed with `DEFAULT_MAX_FETCH_FAILURE_RATE` (50%, deliberately looser than the 10% quarantine ceiling — a fetch failure is expected to be transient/self-healing where a parse failure is evidence of an actual template break, and too tight a ceiling here would make `partial` sticky on an ordinary flaky day, silently stopping closure forever) alongside `quarantineRate` in `discoveryOk`, same denominator, same zero-guard, same override-for-testing shape as the other three guards. Two existing tests needed an explicit `maxFetchFailureRate: 1` override to stay isolated from this new guard (their own subject — `failedCount` bookkeeping and `touchSourceListingSeen` protection — was otherwise incidentally exercising it); a new dedicated test proves the guard trips (`status: 'partial'`) on a systemic all-fetches-fail run while a pre-existing, genuinely-absent listing is left untouched, proving reconciliation never ran off the unreliable run's results.
  - **Reconciliation could commit before the run itself settled.** `expireOverdueListings` and `closeMissingListings` each committed independently (the latter in its own `db.transaction`) BEFORE the final `finishCrawlRun` call that sets `reconciledAt` — a crash or transient DB failure in that window could leave listings closed/expired while the run's own row stayed unsettled (`reconciledAt` still null), or let the catch block relabel an already-reconciled run `'failed'` with stale (pre-reconciliation) counts. Independently judged: real gap, but overstated as a data-corruption risk — chased every consequence and found none reaches actual state corruption: the exclusivity-lock-wedge risk Codex raised is already a documented, accepted Phase 7 gap (round 2 above) that this fix does NOT close either way (a mid-transaction crash still rolls back to `reconciled_at = null`, still wedged — atomicity fixes consistency, not lock recovery); no double-counted missing streaks are possible on a retry (a later run's later `startedAt` re-arms the `lastReconciledAt < run.startedAt` idempotency gate correctly); no baseline poisoning (`discoveredCount`/`vipCount`/`standardCount` are persisted before this window, valid regardless). Taken anyway since the fix is small and removes a real "the row says X, the listings say Y" contradiction. Fixed by extracting `closeMissingListingsInTransaction` (`src/db/reconcile-source-listings.ts`) — same contract as `closeMissingListings`, composable inside a caller's own transaction (the same `DatabaseOrTransaction`-accepting pattern `expireOverdueListings` already used) — and wrapping the entire settlement sequence (terminal-status write → expiry → missing-reconciliation → final status+`reconciledAt` write) in one `db.transaction` in `crawl.ts`. Builds a new local `settledCounts` object rather than mutating the outer `counts` in place: if the transaction rolls back, the outer `catch` block (which marks the run `'failed'` using that same outer `counts`) must never report expiry/closure the database itself rolled back. Proven with a new `crawl.test.ts` scenario: two pre-seeded listings (one past-deadline, one genuinely absent) plus a `db.transaction` stub that lets the first call (`writeSourceListingRevision`) through and fails the second (settlement) — asserts the run is `'failed'` with `expiredCount === 0 && missingCount === 0`, and BOTH pre-seeded listings are untouched. This test fails against the pre-fix code (expiry ran as a separate, unprotected statement before the failure point) and passes against the fix, confirming it actually exercises the regression rather than passing vacuously.

  Proven end-to-end against a fake `HttpFetcher` (synthetic discovery/detail HTML, real Postgres) across 20 scenarios in `src/adapters/jobs-ge/crawl.test.ts`, 9 in `src/adapters/jobs-ge/detail.test.ts`, plus a dedicated DB-layer test in `src/db/ingest.test.ts` proving the lock survives the "status set, not yet reconciled" gap directly — 18 fixes verified across nine review passes (seven per-commit, two whole-branch), each whole-branch pass's findings independently judged (Opus) before any fix landed. One P2 from this pass (bumping `JOBS_GE_DETAIL_PARSER_VERSION` for the new rejection semantics) was skipped per this project's severity-gate policy.

  A tenth review pass — a third `/codex:adversarial-review --base main` run, immediately after the ninth pass's fixes landed — returned 2 more highs, again independently judged (Opus) before acting:
  - **"No completed run exists yet" was treated as "no history exists," letting a truncated crawl certify itself as the baseline.** `getLastCompletedCrawlRun` returning `null` (round 4's "never blocks a source's first-ever run" escape hatch) doesn't actually mean no history exists — a prior `partial` run (e.g. discovery succeeded fully but a detail-fetch storm made that run partial, round 9's own new guard above) still persists a real `discoveredCount`. Without a fallback, a severely truncated crawl (a pagination/caching fault serving one small page everywhere — satisfies `complete` and the fixed floor exactly the way round 4's original scenario did) could certify itself as this source's first `'completed'` run and become the baseline for every later relative-coverage comparison, letting `missingStreakThreshold` (3) repetitions of the same small-page pattern mass-close real listings — reaching the exact failure the relative-coverage guard exists to prevent, just through the "no baseline yet" door instead of around it. Independently judged: confirmed, real, and it lands on the exit gate's own headline guarantee ("incomplete runs cannot mass-close records") — but the exposure window is narrow (only before this source's very first `'completed'` run; once one exists, the original round-4 check already covers it) and the damage is reversible (round 8's `allowReopen` covers `'closed'`, so the next healthy full run reopens anything wrongly closed). Fixed with `getMaxDiscoveredCountForSource` (`src/db/ingest.ts`): the largest `discoveredCount` any `fullCoverage: true` run for this source has ever persisted, regardless of that run's own status (a `partial`/`failed` run's discovered IDs are still a genuine lower bound on corpus size) — used as the baseline only when `lastCompletedRun` is null, leaving a genuinely first-ever run (which has no full-coverage row at all yet) at 0, i.e. still never blocked, exactly preserving round 4's original decision. Deliberately the MAX across history, not the most recent: comparing only against the most recent would just shift the exploit one run later (the truncated run becomes "the most recent" the moment it persists). Deliberately NOT extended to the `vipOk`/`standardOk` per-partition guards (round 5) — those still require a genuine `completed` baseline, since a partial run's per-partition counts are weaker evidence (discovery may have truncated before VIP-bearing pages were even re-observed); left as a documented, narrower scope than the combined-total fix. Proven with a new `crawl.test.ts` scenario (a `partial` run with 10 real listings, then a confirmed 2-listing clamp on the same clock, asserting `'partial'` not `'completed'` and all 10 run-1 listings untouched) verified to fail against the pre-fix logic before restoring the fix, per the round-9 precedent — plus 3 new `ingest.test.ts` unit tests pinning the helper's own contract (0 when no other run exists; max across every status; ignores a `fullCoverage: false` row).
  - **An ambiguous COMMIT could relabel an already-settled run as failed.** PostgreSQL can actually commit round 9's settlement transaction (expiry, closure, counts, `reconciledAt` all persisted) while the client never receives the COMMIT acknowledgment — a standard ambiguous-commit failure class for any client/server transactional system, e.g. a dropped connection at exactly that moment. `db.transaction(...)` still throws on the client side in that case, and the outer `catch` block's unconditional `finishCrawlRun(..., status: 'failed', ...)` would then overwrite the row with a false `'failed'` status and stale, zeroed (pre-settlement) counts — while `source_listings` remains correctly reconciled, recreating in miniature the exact "row says X, listings say Y" contradiction round 9's whole fix was meant to eliminate. Independently judged: confirmed as a real state contradiction, but overstated in consequence (bookkeeping/audit-trail only — no lock wedge, since `reconciledAt` is set either way; no double-counted streaks, since the next run's later `startedAt` still gates correctly; no wrong listing state) — **and, importantly, this is NOT a new regression from round 9's atomicity fix**: `git show` against the pre-round-9 commit shows a byte-for-byte identical unconditional catch block, sitting behind what was then FOUR separate autocommitted settlement steps (each independently vulnerable to the same ambiguous-commit class) rather than round 9's one. Round 9 collapsed four ambiguous-commit windows into one, smaller one — it narrowed this gap rather than introducing it, and this pass found it precisely because the consolidated code was easier to look hard at. Fixed with `failUnsettledCrawlRun` (`src/db/ingest.ts`) — a conditional `WHERE reconciled_at IS NULL` update, replacing the catch block's unconditional `finishCrawlRun` call. `reconciledAt` for a given run id is only ever written by that run's own settlement or this same function, so zero rows updated means the settlement transaction's COMMIT genuinely landed — the row is left exactly as settlement wrote it rather than "resurrected" into a returned success (which would flip the CLI's exit code for a connection failure that genuinely occurred, and could overwrite a row an operator already settled by hand via the documented recovery query, round 8 above). Deliberately a SEPARATE function, not `finishCrawlRun` plus a `WHERE` clause — `finishCrawlRun` throws when its update returns no row, which here would destroy the original mid-run error and report the wrong cause. Proven with a new `crawl.test.ts` scenario that lets the settlement transaction genuinely commit via the real `db` before throwing (simulating the lost acknowledgment, distinct from round 9's own test, which fails the transaction before it ever runs) — asserts the run stays `'completed'` with real `expiredCount`/`missingCount` and `reconciledAt` set, not `'failed'` with zeroed counts — verified to fail against the pre-fix unconditional overwrite before restoring the fix, per the round-9 precedent. Plus 2 new `ingest.test.ts` unit tests pinning `failUnsettledCrawlRun` itself (marks a genuinely unsettled run failed; returns `null` and leaves an already-reconciled run's status/counts/`reconciledAt` completely untouched).

  Proven end-to-end against a fake `HttpFetcher` (synthetic discovery/detail HTML, real Postgres) across 22 scenarios in `src/adapters/jobs-ge/crawl.test.ts` plus 13 DB-layer tests in `src/db/ingest.test.ts` (including 3 for `getMaxDiscoveredCountForSource` and 2 for `failUnsettledCrawlRun`, new this pass, alongside the pre-existing lock/concurrency/settlement-gap tests) and 9 in `src/adapters/jobs-ge/detail.test.ts` — 20 fixes verified across ten review passes (seven per-commit, three whole-branch), each whole-branch pass's findings independently judged (Opus) before any fix landed, and each new regression test in this pass confirmed to fail against its own pre-fix code before the fix was restored.
- [x] Conservative closure logic implemented (an incomplete run cannot mass-close records) — `docs/scraplify-concept.md` §25 amended at merge (2026-09-05) to explicitly narrow Phase 1A's scope: incremental overlap (§10.1's rolling overlap window) is deferred past this phase (moved to §28, "decisions to revisit with evidence") rather than required by it, since jobs.ge's corpus is small and bounded enough (~19 pages) that a full walk every run is the right tradeoff now — revisit only if a later source or corpus growth makes a full walk too slow. Conservative closure alone is what this phase's own exit-gate condition (below) actually requires, and it's fully met and wired into a real crawl (see the orchestrator item above). `src/db/reconcile-source-listings.ts`'s `closeMissingListings` advances missing streaks and closes listings (project decision 2026-09-04: threshold 3 consecutive misses, rejected below 2 since concept §13 requires the first miss to become `missing_suspected`, never `closed`). It takes only a `crawlRunId` and reads that run's `status`/`fullCoverage`/`startedAt`/`sourceId` from its own persisted `crawl_runs` row inside the transaction — deliberately not caller-supplied fields, after adversarial review (2026-09-04) pointed out that trusting a caller's claim about run completeness/coverage would let a caller bug bypass the "incomplete runs cannot mass-close" guarantee entirely. Only `status === 'completed'` **and** `fullCoverage === true` proceeds; anything else is a documented no-op, encoding §13/§21.3's "partial, blocked, or anomalous crawl must not advance missing streaks" as the function's own precondition. `fullCoverage` is a new `crawl_runs` column (migration 0008) set once at `startCrawlRun` time — true only when a run's discovery walked the entire listing space, not an incremental slice. Idempotent per crawl run via a new `source_listings.last_reconciled_at` column (migration 0007): every row `closeMissingListings` touches is gated on `lastReconciledAt < run.startedAt` (or never set) and stamped with `run.startedAt`, so retrying or double-invoking the function for the SAME crawl run can't re-increment `missingStreak` a second time — also caught by adversarial review (2026-09-04); `lastSeenAt` alone doesn't guard against that, since a genuinely-missing listing's `lastSeenAt` never changes between repeat calls for one run. `expireOverdueListings` handles deadline-based expiry separately and unconditionally, since a passed deadline is a definitive source-stated fact, not an absence inferred from crawl coverage — callers must run it before `closeMissingListings` so an expired listing's status change excludes it from missing-streak accounting for free. §10.1's incremental discovery (a rolling overlap window that can stop early once several consecutive pages contain only known, unchanged records) is not implemented — deferred, since jobs.ge's corpus is small and bounded enough (~19 pages, `RECON_NOTES.md`) that the orchestrator just walks every page every run and sets `fullCoverage: true` unconditionally; the overlap-window optimization can be added later without changing this module's contract.
- [x] Local read-only run capability implemented — `src/cli/run-jobs-ge-crawl.ts` is a one-shot entry point wiring the orchestrator to a real `HttpFetcher`/`RateLimiter` (per-source rate limit from `jobsGePolicy`, `isJobsGeUrlAllowed` as the redirect-safe allow-list, `DEFAULT_MISSING_STREAK_THRESHOLD` = 3 per the project decision above) and a real Postgres connection; run via `npm run crawl:jobs-ge` after `npm run build`. Does the database-reachability preflight concept §19.1 requires (`select 1`, failing loudly rather than silently skipping a run) — the configuration and source-policy preflights it also requires are already enforced at import time (`DATABASE_URL` required by `db/client.ts`; the policy's own Zod `.parse()`), and the lock preflight is `startCrawlRun`'s existing exclusivity index, surfaced here as a treated-as-benign `CrawlAlreadyRunningError` skip. `scripts/run-jobs-ge-crawl.ps1` wraps it for Windows Task Scheduler (captures output Task Scheduler wouldn't on its own, to `logs/`, preserves the real exit code) and `scripts/register-jobs-ge-schedule.ps1` registers the recurring task (default 1440 min/nightly — corrected from an initial 60-min default that cited the wrong row of concept §19.2's cadence table; see the eighth adversarial-review pass above) — written, hand-verified against a real Postgres instance, but deliberately **not registered**. `docs/scraplify-concept.md` §25 amended at merge (2026-09-05) to make this item's actual scope explicit: implementing the code-level capability to schedule local runs, not necessarily having a live schedule already registered — activating one is a separate operator action, taken deliberately whenever the project owner is ready, not a code deliverable this phase's exit gate depends on. Checked off on that basis — the code-level capability is complete and tested; run `./scripts/register-jobs-ge-schedule.ps1` whenever ready to start live recurring runs.

  **Fixed (originally a Codex review P2, 2026-09-05, deferred; fixed at merge after blocking the exit-gate closure attempt below):** `register-jobs-ge-schedule.ps1`'s scheduled action ran `powershell.exe -NoProfile` wrapping a bare `node` call. On a machine where Node is put on `PATH` by fnm's PowerShell profile hook (this project's own documented setup), `-NoProfile` meant an unattended Task Scheduler firing wouldn't resolve `node` the same way an interactive shell does — registration itself would succeed, but every scheduled run could fail. Fixed by resolving `node.exe`'s absolute path in `register-jobs-ge-schedule.ps1` itself (always run interactively, in a profile-loaded shell where fnm's hook has already run) via `(Get-Command node).Source`, then passing it through to `run-jobs-ge-crawl.ps1` as a new `-NodePath` parameter (default `'node'`, for direct/manual invocation outside Task Scheduler) baked into the registered task's own arguments — so an unattended run never depends on `-NoProfile`'s PATH at all.

  This surfaced a second, independent bug in the same fix's first attempt: both `.ps1` scripts contained em dashes with no UTF-8 BOM, and Windows PowerShell 5.1 (the exact `powershell.exe` the scheduled action invokes) decodes a BOM-less file using the system's legacy code page rather than assuming UTF-8, turning the em dash inside `run-jobs-ge-crawl.ps1`'s executed `throw` string into a parse error — every scheduled run would have failed before ever reaching `-NodePath`. Caught by the per-commit gate, not by this session's own first verification attempt: parsing the file's content as an already-decoded UTF-8 string (`Get-Content -Encoding UTF8` piped into the parser) reported no error, because that sidesteps the exact legacy-codepage decoding the real `powershell.exe -File` invocation performs — only reproducing Codex's own exact repro command (`powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File scripts/run-jobs-ge-crawl.ps1`) actually revealed it. Fixed by removing every non-ASCII character from both `.ps1` scripts (plain `-` instead of em dashes, "section N.N" instead of `§N.N`) rather than relying on a BOM surviving every future edit/tool — re-verified against the real `powershell.exe -File` invocation path this time, not just a parsed-string proxy for it.
- [x] Concurrency-safe revision write protocol implemented and tested (carried forward from Phase 0, see above)

**Exit gate condition (concept §25):** jobs.ge reruns idempotently; new, changed, unchanged, missing, expired, and failed states are correct; incomplete runs cannot mass-close records.
